That second channel is a discretization artefact: the staircase resonates with the secular motion when J R_DAC = f_z for integer J, observed for J = 8 to 14, that is 450 down to 257.1 kHz at f_z = 3.6 MHz, all within the 500 kHz hardware cap while J = 7 would need 514.3 kHz, and the apparatus ran at R_DAC = 480 kHz, f_z/R_DAC = 7.5, maximally incommensurate in the J = 7 to 8 gap **[verified]**, **[recomputed here]**. Model it *deterministically* - sample z₀(t) at R_DAC, filter it, and let unitary evolution produce the excitation - never as a Lindblad rate, and note there is no fixed advance per update: 5 µm is the grid spacing, the realized advances being 11.1, 8.2 and 6.4 µm **[corrected]**, **[recomputed here]**. With megasample-per-second generators no harmonic J ≥ 1 lands on the secular frequency, so it is optional, not fundamental **[verified]**.

**Schedule, wall clock and the circuit-level error model.** A QCCD schedule charges transport as 1.10 Σ_p N_p τ_p, the factor being the stated interpolation-and-electronics overhead, with per-primitive durations 57.8 µs (intrazone shift), 282.6 µs (interzone shift), 128.2 µs (split/combine) and 200.0 µs (swap) **[verified]** (Pino et al. 2021, Tables II-III). Cooling is charged *per two-qubit-gate slot*, one Doppler-plus-sideband block (550 + 850 µs) and one pre-gate axial-sideband stage (650 µs), never per transport primitive: the paper's own time budgets recover that cadence exactly, and charging 1400 µs per primitive overestimates a four-qubit quantum-volume circuit's cooling time about ninefold **[corrected]**. The tabulated per-primitive "axial heat" and "radial heat" columns are *temperatures expressed as frequencies*, k_B T/h in MHz, not quanta; convert with the full Bose-Einstein expression n̄ = [exp(ν/ν_T) − 1]⁻¹, so the ν_T = 2 MHz swap row gives n̄ = 1.6021 at ν = 0.97 MHz (T = 96.0 µK), while dividing the tabulated value by the mode frequency is the Rayleigh-Jeans shortcut and returns approximately n̄ + ½ because k_B T/(hν) is only 1.4 to 2.1 here **[corrected]**, **[recomputed here]**. Because one common temperature is fitted every mode takes its own occupation, there is no crystal-wide n̄, and the source never says whether the tabulated heat is an absolute post-operation temperature or a per-operation increment, so it must not be assumed to accumulate **[corrected]**. At the circuit level the published model applies a depolarizing channel after each two-qubit gate with a rate from two-qubit randomized benchmarking, and *transport failures were removed from the data rather than modelled*, so they are heralded erasure and never a depolarizing term **[corrected]**; the mode-structure probe used to catch loss and reordering is explicitly blind to qubit-ion swaps and to multiple reorderings that restore the correct order before the probe, a known undetected-error path with no bound.

**Readout forward models: how the sources measure the excitation this module predicts.** The resolved-sideband lineshape is r_m(Δ_m) = Σ_{n≥m} ρ_nn [4|g_nm|²/Ω_nm²] sin²(Ω_nm t_m/2) with Ω_nm = √(4|g_nm|² + Δ_m²), built from g_m = (−iη)^m g₀/m! and g_nm = g_m √(n!/(n − m)!), so g_n1 = −iηg₀√n and g_n2 = −½η²g₀√(n(n−1)) **[verified]** (Sterk et al. 2022, Eq. 2 and Methods). g₀ is *not* the carrier Rabi frequency: in this sin²(Ωt/2) convention g₀ = Ω_carrier/2 and the sideband π-time is π/(2|g_nm|), so reading g₀ as a Rabi frequency is a factor 2 in every probe time, and the sum starting at n = m makes r_m vanish identically for a pure ground state **[corrected]**. Because r_m depends only on the post-transport Fock populations it is blind to motional coherences and hence to motional phase, but *not* to the diagonal's shape: the claim that it cannot separate coherent from thermal excitation at equal mean quanta is wrong, since the couplings are n-dependent and the second-order sideband weights ⟨n(n−1)⟩ = n̄² (Poissonian) against 2n̄² (Bose-Einstein) **[corrected]**. Two pseudo-energy metrics serve as optimizer objectives, neither in quanta and neither monotonic in mean occupation. The closed-loop loss ℒ(X) = max_h [α₁ ∫_{B₁} (dΔ₁/2π) r₁ + (α₂ ∫_{B₂} (dΔ₂/2π) r₂)²] has α₁ = α₂ = 2 kHz⁻¹ with the second weight *inside* the square, which keeps the term dimensionless and weights the second moment more heavily; it is a worst case over hold offsets, never a mean, and is not invertible above its fold (10² to 10³ quanta at short probe times, 10 to 10² at long ones), because once |g_nm| t_m exceeds π the sin² factor stops growing - "monotonically" is an extraction artefact **[corrected]**. Walther's E_p = 2(P_{↑,+1}(3π) − P_{↑,−1}(3π) − P_{↑,−2}(2π)) + 7/2 uses *carrier-referenced* pulse areas and η-specific constants that zero it at the ground state (−0.0144 at η = 0.23), is single-valued only below about 50 quanta, and its inversion curve is published only as a plot; its companion Rabi fit references the area to the *bare* carrier, so traces are never normalized to the observed carrier **[corrected]**, **[recomputed here]** (Walther et al. 2012, Eqs. 1-3). Finally, the independent-energy convolution p_n = Σ_{m=0}^{n} [n̄_th^m/(n̄_th + 1)^{m+1}] e^{−n̄_α} n̄_α^{n−m}/(n − m)! and the exact displaced thermal state differ in variance by exactly 2 n̄_th n̄_α, the printed index range m = n to N being a typo on a Gamma pole for m > n; use one model in both the forward pass and the fit or the extracted n̄_α is biased **[corrected]**, **[recomputed here]**.

**How this attaches to the plan's existing objects.** The Section 4.1.2 crystal solver already computes equilibrium positions and the Coulomb Hessian; for splitting it is called at every mesh point of a time-dependent well whose external potential is quartic rather than harmonic, with the mode decomposition from the Hessian of eΦ_tot in the (x₀, d) coordinates rather than the Section 4.1.3 harmonic tables. The Section 4.1.5 heating model is reused unchanged - one single-sided S_E(ω) with Γ_h = e²S_E/(4mħω) drives all three channels, made time-dependent through ω(t) along the trajectory - and the equal-rate pair {√Γ a, √Γ a†} the transport sources use is the N̄ ≫ 1 limit of the plan's Γ(N̄+1)/ΓN̄ pair, not a competing convention; importing it with a finite N̄ adds unphysical damping over microseconds. The Section 4.1.6 surface-electrode layer supplies the φ̃_n basis a real waveform solve needs; the Section 4.1.1 pseudopotential Ψ = Q²|E_rf|²/(4mΩ²) in joules is q φ_ps in volts, the same peak convention; and the Section 6.7 collision channel already carries reorder permutations, which is where a transport failure or an undetected ion swap enters as a herald, its idle-time evolution being what a dwell at a junction centre or a distal zone is. The Section 7.3 scheduler gains transport primitives with their durations, the 1.10 overhead and the per-slot cooling cadence. The Section 3.3 `Trap` object gains *time-dependent voltages*: today it holds either explicit secular frequencies or a static (V_rf, Ω_rf, U_dc, geometry) tuple, and M12 makes the dc voltages a schedule V_n(t) delivered through the Section 7.10 hardware chain (zero-order hold at the DAC step, the digital anti-alias filter, then the analog low-pass as a transfer function), with x_well(t) and ω(t) derived from the *filtered* voltages, never from the commanded staircase. Appendix E's `Transport`, `Waveform` and `Zone` records are the public surface.

**What the sources do not supply.** Nine gaps, each an M12 design decision the plan makes for itself and tags **[background]**, and each carried into Section 12: no merge model anywhere, so nothing states whether the time reverse of a split is optimal, how the pre-merge relative motional phase of the two wells enters, or how merge excitation scales; no zero-excitation diabatic protocol, only the Fourier criterion; no quantum dynamics through the splitting critical point, every treatment being classical with quanta obtained post hoc as ΔE/(ħω_f), a ceiling rather than a state, and no second-quantized two-ion Hamiltonian, no mode structure for a purely quartic well and no squeezing from the collapse to ω_CP; no electrode basis potentials in any source, so the dominant real-world excitation channel, residual force and curvature from finite electrode structure, has no quantitative model and the +3 mV offset that drives n̄ above 15 cannot be predicted; no bridge from residual quanta to gate error, nothing mapping a residual n̄ or coherent displacement onto Mølmer-Sørensen or light-shift infidelity, onto dephasing accumulated during a shuttle, or onto a Kraus channel; no mixed-species transport or splitting, only the statement that a different coolant species needs special consideration because all modes are excited during both diabatic transport and separation; no micromotion in either splitting framework, both putting the ions on the rf node with tight radial confinement throughout so that neither contains a pseudopotential term at all; an adiabatic-suppression constant undefined in its source; and no prescription for the inter-segment hand-off of the Ermakov state (ρ, ρ̇, μ), the accumulated squeezing (δ, θ) and the coherent phase across a transport, a split, a junction and a merge, which is what accumulating squeezing over a multi-segment schedule requires.

---


# Part III. Numerics, noise, control and readout

## 5. Hilbert space, frames and numerical strategy

### 5.1 Composite space

Each ion is a qudit of dimension d. Default d = 2 (the qubit pair). When photon scattering or imperfect optical pumping is enabled, d grows to include the leakage levels the species module marks as reachable (for a 171Yb+ hyperfine qubit: the two other F = 1 Zeeman states at minimum; for optical qubits: the other D-state Zeeman sublevels populated by decay). Each motional mode is a truncated oscillator. The space is the tensor product of ions then modes; operators are built once per `HilbertSpace` and cached.

Two truncation schemes are supported and selectable per run:

- **Per-mode cutoff** n_max,m, the highest Fock index kept, so that a mode's dimension is d_m = n_max,m + 1 and the motional dimension is Π_m d_m (Section 11 quotes d_m). Simple, exact for any product of per-mode populations, wasteful when many modes are cold.
- **Excitation-number restriction (ENR):** total excitation across a group of modes capped at N_exc, using QuTiP's `enr_state_dictionaries`, `enr_destroy`, `enr_fock`, `enr_thermal_dm` (present in 5.3.1). For M modes near the ground state this reduces the motional dimension from (n_max+1)^M to C(M + N_exc, N_exc). The displacement operators D(iη) needed by the Hamiltonian are built inside the ENR space from the ENR ladder operators by matrix exponential, so no operator is ever formed in the unrestricted space; Section 5.1.1 gives the measured accuracy of that construction.

The default for joint-exact runs is per-mode cutoffs for the modes a pulse resolves dynamically (its driven modes and the spectators selected by the contribution criterion of Section 11.3), with the remaining modes carried as frozen spectators (Section 5.2), because a product space is what the exact displacement operator, the mode-factorized kernel of Section 11.3 and the boundary monitor all assume. ENR is an option for a group of cold, undriven modes that must nevertheless be carried dynamically, with the caveats of Section 5.1.1 and three more measured on 5.3.1: `ptrace` on any space containing an ENR factor raises `NotImplementedError`, so an ENR run cannot produce the reduced qubit state that `Result.spam`, the channel summary and GATE_LOCAL tomography need, or a per-mode reduced density matrix, unless the module builds the marginal itself from `enr_state_dictionaries`, which it does; `tensor(sigmap(), D_enr)` reports `dims` whose product (98 for two modes at N_exc = 6) disagrees with its `shape` (56), so every dimension computation reads `shape` behind an assertion and never `dims`; and the ENR displacement is unitary to 8 × 10⁻¹⁵ while silently wrong near the cap (⟨n₀⟩ = 2.2412 against 2.25 after a displacement α = 1.5 at N_exc = 6), which is why its cost is quoted at N_exc ≈ 2n_phys with the top-shell population as the only convergence diagnostic (2026-09-04 numerics critique) **[corrected: critique, 2026-09-04]**: an ENR space is not a product space, so the exponential of its sum generator differs from the product of per-mode exponentials near the excitation cap, it cannot be applied mode by mode, and the factorized kernel does not apply to it. A Mølmer-Sørensen gate transiently displaces its target mode by |α| up to about 1, which that mode's cap must accommodate with the margin of Section 5.1.1; the first version of this plan made ENR over one axis the default, which the 2026-09-04 critique showed to be incompatible with the factorized kernel and with the two-family closure of Section 4.4.7.

#### 5.1.1 Constructing the displacement operator in a truncated space

Two constructions of D(iη) exist in a space of n_max Fock levels, and they are not the same matrix. The **matrix exponential of the truncated generator**, expm[iη(a + a†)] with the truncated a (QuTiP's `displace`, and likewise the ENR ladder operators in an ENR space), is exactly unitary (defect below 10⁻¹⁵ in every case measured) but its matrix elements near the truncation boundary are wrong. The **analytic elements** ⟨n′|D(iη)|n⟩ = e^{−η²/2} (n_<!/n_>!)^{1/2} (iη)^{|n′−n|} L^{(|n′−n|)}_{n_<}(η²), the exact infinite-space elements of Section 4.3.1 written into the truncated matrix, are exact element by element (the modulus in the exponent is specific to a purely imaginary displacement, for which the lower branch's (−α*)^{n−n′} equals (iη)^{n−n′}; for a general complex α the two branches are α^{n′−n} where n′ > n, below the diagonal of the matrix ⟨n′|·|n⟩ with row n′ and column n, and (−α*)^{n−n′} where n′ < n, above it (the second revision had the two labels interchanged; derivation audit of 2026-09-04); the first version of this plan printed n′ − n without the modulus, which is wrong above the diagonal by (iη)^{−2|n′−n|} and was caught by the 2026-09-04 critique, the benchmark script having used the correct branches), but the truncated matrix is not unitary: its top column loses 9% of its norm at n_max = 10 and η = 0.1, 30% at n_max = 40, and about half at η ≥ 0.5, so a state that reached the boundary would lose norm rather than be reflected. Measured on this machine on 2026-09-04 (`bench_numerics.py`, Appendix D) **[recomputed here]**:

| d_m (Fock levels) | η | max element difference, block n, n′ < d_m/2 | analytic norm loss, column d_m/2 |
|---|---|---|---|
| 8 | 0.1 / 0.5 / 1.0 | 2 × 10⁻¹³ / 2 × 10⁻⁶ / 1 × 10⁻³ | 3 × 10⁻⁸ / 6 × 10⁻³ / 0.3 |
| 10 | 0.1 / 0.5 / 1.0 | 3 × 10⁻¹⁶ / 7 × 10⁻⁸ / 2 × 10⁻⁴ | 2 × 10⁻¹⁰ / 1 × 10⁻³ / 0.18 |
| 20 | 0.1 / 0.5 / 1.0 | 1 × 10⁻¹⁶ / 1 × 10⁻¹⁵ / 3 × 10⁻⁹ | 1 × 10⁻¹⁶ / 3 × 10⁻⁸ / 3 × 10⁻³ |
| 40 | 0.1 / 0.5 / 1.0 | 7 × 10⁻¹⁶ / 8 × 10⁻¹⁶ / 7 × 10⁻¹⁶ | 1 × 10⁻¹⁵ / 4 × 10⁻¹⁶ / 3 × 10⁻⁹ |

In an ENR space of two modes, the exponential of the ENR sum generator Σ_m iη_m(a_m + a_m†) agrees with the analytic elements, for states with n₁ + n₂ ≤ N_exc/2, to 2 × 10⁻¹⁰, 6 × 10⁻¹⁶ and 2 × 10⁻¹⁶ at η = 0.1 and to 1 × 10⁻⁶, 4 × 10⁻¹⁰ and 7 × 10⁻¹⁶ at η = 0.3 for N_exc = 6, 10 and 16, while remaining unitary to 10⁻¹⁵. That exponential of the sum generator is the operator definition in an ENR space: because the space is not a tensor product, the per-mode generators do not commute there (maximum commutator element 0.33 at N_exc = 10, η = 0.3 and 0.2, against exactly 0 in the product space) and the product of per-mode exponentials is a different operator near the cap (maximum element difference 1.3 × 10⁻² for the three modes of Section 11.1 at N_exc = 10, 7 × 10⁻¹⁶ inside n_total ≤ N_exc/2; 2026-09-04 critique, measured on 5.3.1), so the product form, which is the physical operator and the one the factorized kernel of Section 11.3 applies, is used in product spaces only, and the two are never swapped as an optimization. The rules the code follows: (i) the operator in the Hamiltonian is the matrix exponential, in the per-mode or the ENR space, because unitarity is what keeps the norm and the boundary monitor meaningful; (ii) the analytic elements are the test oracle: a `HilbertSpace(modes, expected_n_range, η_max)` asserts at construction that the interior elements of its exponentials match them over the declared expected occupation range to a tolerance read from the table above (10⁻¹² where the table reaches it, the table's own value otherwise), the truncation monitor of Section 5.5 reports when the actual population exceeds the declared range, and the margins the table implies, about 6 levels at η ≤ 0.1, 10 at η ≤ 0.5 and 20 at η ≈ 1, are stored as a fixture with interpolation in η rather than as a rule of thumb; (iii) the Rabi-frequency table Ω_{n′,n} of Section 4.3.1 and every closed-form test are evaluated from the analytic form, never from the exponential; (iv) the boundary monitor of Section 5.5 is what makes either construction trustworthy, since both are wrong at the boundary.

The exact displacement operator is dense within each mode's Fock space: |n⟩ couples to every |n′⟩ with weight of order η^{|n′−n|}(n_>!/n_<!)^{1/2}/|n′ − n|!, and the elements above 10⁻¹⁰ reach beyond |n′ − n| = 8 for η = 0.1 at n ≈ 8. The drive operator σ₊^i ⊗ Π_m D_m therefore has 2^{N−1} Π_m n_max,m² non-zeros, that is Π_m n_max,m/2 per row rather than "a few", and this, not the step count, is what sets the cost model of Section 11. Sparsifying by dropping elements below a threshold is an approximation of the truncation kind and is offered with the dropped weight reported, but at n_max ≈ 8 and η ≈ 0.1 it removes almost nothing; the structural remedy is to apply D as a product of mode-local factors (Section 11.3).

### 5.2 Frames and the form of the Hamiltonian

The simulator works in the frame rotating at each ion's qubit frequency (optical rotating-wave approximation) but in the **Schrödinger picture for the motion**. In this frame the free Hamiltonian is H_0 = Σ_m ħω_m a_m†a_m (plus any qubit-frequency offsets that are being modelled as static detunings), and every drive on ion i is a term of the form

H_drive,i(t) = (ħ/2) Σ_tones Ω_i,tone(t) e^{−i(μ_tone t − φ_tone(t))} σ_+^{(i)} ⊗ D_i(Δk) + h.c.,  φ_tone ≡ Δk·X̄_i − Δφ_i,

where D_i(Δk) = Π_m D_m(iη_im) is the product of displacement operators over all modes with the ion's Lamb-Dicke parameters for the drive's effective wavevector, μ_tone is the tone's detuning from the carrier (Section 13), and φ_tone is the tone's phase in the ion's frame: the beam-phase difference Δφ_i offset by the geometric phase Δk·X̄_i of the ion's equilibrium position, which is the e^{+i(Δk·X̄_i − Δφ_i)} of Section 13 and the e^{i(δkX̄_i − μ_i t − δφ_i)} of Section 4.3.1 written as one symbol; Δφ_i is defined as the phase of the beat note written E ∝ cos(ω_L t − Δk·r + Δφ_i), so that for a Raman pair with Δk = k₁ − k₂ and beam fields E_j ∝ cos(k_j·r − ω_j t + φ_j) it is Δφ_i = φ₂ − φ₁ (the relative sign of Δk·X̄_i and the optical phase is physical, the field depending only on their sum in the other ordering, and a virtual RZ(θ) shifts every later φ_tone by φ → φ − θ, the time-order rule of Section 7.6 and the one pinned sign, tested on a concrete sequence (RZ(0.1) then GPi2(0) on |0⟩ against independently multiplied matrices, Section 9.6) rather than by asserting that a rule is pinned; the second revision printed +θ here against 7.6's −θ, which the 2026-09-04 architect critique caught, and the derivation audit of 2026-09-04 had found the definition missing) **[corrected: derivation audit, 2026-09-04]**. A native gate's phase parameter φ (Section 7.6) is φ_tone in the qubit's phase frame; the virtual-Z rule, the MS spin phase of Section 4.4.2 and the vector addition of crosstalk drives in Section 6.6 all refer to this one sign, and the Section 9.6 test that GPi2(0) on |0⟩ produces the state of the Section 7.6 matrix pins it. The reasons for this choice, rather than the interaction picture with respect to the motion:

- The operator part σ_+ ⊗ D_i is **time independent**, so it is built once (a sparse matrix) and the only time dependence is a complex scalar coefficient per tone, which is exactly what QuTiP's `QobjEvo([H0, [A, c(t)]])` handles efficiently.
- The exact displacement operator includes the carrier, all sidebands, and all Debye-Waller factors automatically; nothing is expanded in η.
- Off-resonant terms (carrier during a sideband drive, spectator modes, counter-rotating sideband terms) are present without special handling. Their neglect, when a user wants the textbook model, is done by the builder's `lamb_dicke_order` and `rwa` options, which replace D_i by its expansion to a given order and keep only tones near a chosen resonance.
- The cost is that H_0 sets the fastest timescale: the component |n⟩ of the state rotates at nω_m, so the stiffness grows with the truncation cap. Measured with DOP853 at rtol 10⁻⁸ and atol 10⁻¹⁰ (`bench_numerics.py`, Appendix D) **[recomputed here]**, a 2 µs carrier π pulse on a 3 MHz mode costs 1.3 × 10³ right-hand-side evaluations at n_max = 6 and 3.2 × 10³ at n_max = 41, and a 10 µs detuned spin-dependent force 5.1 × 10³ and 1.6 × 10⁴: a factor 2.5 to 3 for a sevenfold larger cap, because the error control weighs the small high-n amplitudes lightly. That is 14 to 45 steps per period of the mode, at 12 evaluations per DOP853 step, so a 100 µs gate on a 3 MHz mode needs 4 × 10³ to 1.3 × 10⁴ steps. The alternative, the interaction picture with respect to H_0, replaces σ₊ ⊗ D by Σ_k e^{ikω_m t} σ₊ ⊗ A_k over the 2n_max + 1 sideband operators A_k (the elements of D with n′ − n = k); it holds the evaluation count flat (0.6 × 10³ and 3.7 × 10³ to 4.2 × 10³ in the same tests, agreeing with the Schrödinger-picture state to 10⁻⁸) but needs 2n_max + 1 operators per drive term, Π_m(2n_max,m + 1) for M modes, and ran 5 to 10 times slower in wall time at n_max = 41. It is therefore the option `frame="interaction"`, whose useful form truncates the sideband sum at |k| ≤ k_max and reports the dropped weight Σ_{|k|>k_max}‖A_k‖, an approximation of the truncation kind validated against the default; the default stays the Schrödinger picture. Section 11 gives the measured wall times.

Coefficients are supplied as Python functions with the QuTiP 5.3 signature `f(t, **kwargs)` or as sampled NumPy arrays with `tlist` (cubic spline by default, `order=0` for step functions); string coefficients are avoided because compiling them requires Cython, which is optional in this environment, and the fallback is a slow `eval`.

When a laser phase noise realization or an amplitude noise realization is part of a shot, it enters as a sampled array coefficient, so the same builder serves noisy and noiseless pulses.

**Spectator modes that are not resolved dynamically.** A mode may be frozen for a pulse when the closed-form residual displacement and entangling-angle contribution of the Section 4.4.3 integrals, evaluated with the pulse's detuning schedule and the mode's n̄, fall below the run's tolerances, |α_m(τ)|²(2n̄_m + 1) below the residual-displacement tolerance and |χ_m(τ)| below a freeze tolerance that is looser than Section 11.3's dropped-mode rule and distinct from it: every mode is assigned to exactly one of three classes, dropped when |α_m|²(2n̄_m + 1) < 10⁻⁶ and |χ_m| < 10⁻⁴ (nothing absorbs a dropped mode's loss), frozen when above that pair but with |χ_m| below the miscalibration the entangling-gate calibration of Section 7.5 absorbs (default 0.05 rad, the χ_m loss folded into the calibration target and reported per mode), and resolved otherwise, the class recorded per mode in `Diagnostics.mode_class`; the second revision said freezing shared the dropped-mode criterion while describing a frozen spectator that loses 0.03 rad of χ, 300 times the angle tolerance, so that freezing was unreachable as written and with it the default of Section 5.4 (2026-09-04 architect and numerics critiques) **[corrected: critique, 2026-09-04]**; the detuning guard |μ_tone − ℓω_m| > 20 η_m Ω √(n_m + 1) for all ℓ ≠ 0 is kept only as a sanity check whose implied off-resonant excitation (1/20)² = 2.5 × 10⁻³ per sideband is reported. The first version of this plan used the guard as the criterion, which is both too loose (7 × 10⁻² summed over fifteen spectators against the 10⁻⁶ threshold Section 11.3 demands of a dropped mode) and never satisfied in a long chain, whose neighbouring transverse modes lie 10 to 160 kHz apart against a 286 kHz window at n̄ = 20 with the numbers below (caught by the 2026-09-04 experimentalist critique) **[corrected]**. When the criterion holds the mode's state does not change during the pulse beyond the off-resonant excitation, and the drive sees only the Debye-Waller operator D_m projected onto the mode's Fock state, e^{−η_m²/2} L_{n_m}(η_m²) (Section 4.3.1). The option `spectators="frozen"` removes such modes from the joint space, samples n_m once per shot from the mode's thermal distribution, and multiplies Ω_i by the exact Debye-Waller factor of that Fock state: Wineland 1998's frozen-spectator statistics (Eqs. 122-127, Section 4.2.7) made an engine option. Its error bound is the summed off-resonant excitation probability (η_m Ω √(n_m + 1)/(μ − ℓω_m))² over the nearest sidebands, reported together with the frozen mode's closed-form residual displacement α_m(τ) and its contribution χ_m(τ) to the entangling angle from the Section 4.4.3 integrals, because for a two-qubit gate the leading effect of freezing a mode is the loss of χ_m, a coherent miscalibration of first order in the coupling (about 0.03 rad, 4% of π/4, for η = 0.05, Ω = 2π × 62.5 kHz, δ_m = 2π × 100 kHz and τ = 100 µs), which the calibration target absorbs so that a frozen-spectator run is calibrated to the same entangling angle as the joint run; Section 9.9 tests χ and the final probabilities against the fully joint run. It is what makes Doppler-cooled spectator modes (n̄ ≈ 10 to 20) affordable at all, since resolving them dynamically would need n_max ≈ 100 per mode; it applies to the modes of other families and to axial modes far from the tones, not to the transverse spectators of a long chain within tens of kilohertz of the tones, which cannot be frozen and must be resolved, which is why long chains run in GATE_LOCAL with the mode set the contribution criterion of Section 11.3 selects.

### 5.3 Solvers

- **Pure-state evolution** (`sesolve`) whenever the run has no continuous dissipation to represent, or when dissipation is handled by quantum trajectories. Integrator: `dop853` by default with atol 10⁻¹⁰, rtol 10⁻⁸ (tightened by the convergence check) and `nsteps` raised to 10⁷; it was 10 to 25% faster than `vern9` on the Mølmer-Sørensen benchmark of Section 11 with final states agreeing to 6 × 10⁻⁷ in norm (`bench_ms_timing_v4.py`; the second revision said 'identical', which at these tolerances means 10⁻⁷ and not 10⁻¹⁵), while `adams` (scipy's zvode multistep method) aborts with "excess work" at n_max ≈ 40 unless `nsteps` is raised and is not used for oscillatory Hamiltonians **[recomputed here]**. The adaptive step density measured in Section 5.2, 14 to 45 steps per period of the highest mode, is the budget; the output grid is separate and only needs to resolve what is stored. The measured failure mode is stiffness at large caps: with atol 10⁻¹⁰ `dop853` aborts ("problem is probably stiff") at d_m = 121 on the Section 11.1 pulse while succeeding at 101, and succeeds at 121 with atol 10⁻⁸ or with `vern9` (2026-09-04 critique, checked on 5.3.1), so the solver applies an escalation ladder `dop853` → `vern9` → a larger `max_step` budget at atol 10⁻⁸, and never a multistep method: QuTiP's `bdf` is scipy's zvode with a dense finite-difference Jacobian (D extra right-hand sides per Jacobian and an O(D³) factorization, 67 MB at D = 2048), and the failure is not stiffness anyway, since the spectrum of −iH lies on the imaginary axis, where BDF of order 3 and above is unstable and BDF2 strongly damped, so a damping multistep rung would destroy unitarity on exactly the oscillatory problem it is asked to rescue; atol is keyed to the measured points, 10⁻¹⁰ up to d_m ≈ 100 and 10⁻⁸ above, validated at d_m = 121, 151 and 201 (the second revision's rule atol ≈ 10⁻¹⁰ × d_m/50 returned 2.4 × 10⁻¹⁰ at the very case that motivated it, 40 times tighter than the 10⁻⁸ measured to clear the abort, so the ladder as written still aborted there; 2026-09-04 numerics critique) **[corrected: critique, 2026-09-04]**; the solver records the integrator used in the diagnostics and reports a tightened convergence run that aborts rather than losing it.
- **Density-matrix evolution** (`mesolve`) for small spaces (dimension below about 50 to 100, the Liouvillian below 10⁴ × 10⁴). This is the reference mode for validating heating, dephasing and scattering channels and a reference-only path, not a default: one Liouvillian application costs about 2D times one H·ψ, the measured `mesolve`/`sesolve` wall-time ratio at dimension 64 on the Section 11.1 Hamiltonian is 69.5, and with the operator construction the v3 benchmark used (the exponential of the joint generator, which returns a Dense `Qobj`) `liouvillian` returns Dense, 130 GB at D = 300, so the drive operator is built as a tensor product of per-mode exponentials (CSR with exactly the predicted non-zeros, `bench_ms_timing_v4.py`) with an explicit `.to("CSR")` guard before any `liouvillian` call (the second revision put the boundary at 300 and made `mesolve` the default there; 2026-09-04 numerics critique) **[corrected: critique, 2026-09-04]**; hot-mode cases such as a Doppler-cooled n̄ ≈ 20 need d_m ≳ 150 for a boundary population below 10⁻⁴ and run as trajectories.
- **Quantum trajectories** (`mcsolve`, `improved_sampling=True`, parallel `map`) for larger spaces with Lindblad channels: heating quanta, scattering events and leakage appear as jumps; the ensemble over trajectories and over quasi-static noise samples is the physical ensemble that shots are drawn from. With `improved_sampling=True` QuTiP evolves the no-jump trajectory once as a deterministic member carrying weight p_no-jump and gives the stochastic trajectories the residual weight, so shots must be drawn from the weighted mixture (`deterministic_weight_info`), never uniformly from the stored trajectories, which would bias per-shot observables by the no-jump probability (0.62 against the correct 0.32 on a damped-oscillator check; 2026-09-04 critique); the per-trajectory seeds are passed explicitly as a list spawned from the run's root seed (Section 3.4).
- **Piecewise propagators** (`propagator` with `piecewise_t`) only on internal-state-only spaces (Section 11.3 item 6), where the dimension is small and one propagator serves many initial states; on the joint space a propagator is a dense object costing D state evolutions and the calibration scans vary H rather than the initial state, so none is built there, and because `piecewise_t` requires a piecewise-constant generator, segmenting a smooth envelope for it is a declared approximation whose segmentation error is reported.
- **Floquet** (`FloquetBasis`, `fmmesolve`) is reserved for the micromotion module when the rf drive is included explicitly (Section 4.1 option); it is not on the default path.
- **Bloch-Redfield** (`brmesolve`) is available for colored-noise dephasing where a spectral density rather than a white Lindblad rate is the right description (magnetic-field noise with a known S_B(ω)); it is an option for static-H idle intervals and for internal-state-only spaces (the optical Bloch layer of Section 4.2.8), never inside a driven pulse, where the Bloch-Redfield tensor would have to be rebuilt in the dressed basis at every step and the secular approximation fails for a strongly driven system; during pulses coloured noise enters as the sampled time series of Section 6.1(d).

### 5.4 Fidelity levels

- **JOINT_EXACT.** The full state of all N ions and all M included modes is evolved through every pulse. This is the only mode with no approximation beyond truncation. Practical range, from the measured cost model of Section 11: a joint dimension of about 2 × 10³, that is two or three ions with two or three dynamically resolved modes, or more ions with one or two resolved modes and frozen spectators, at minutes per entangling pulse, with a hard ceiling set by the drive operator's memory (Section 11.5); the range "N ≤ 8 under ENR" of the first version of this plan is withdrawn, since at N = 8 with eight axial modes and N_exc = 6 the drive operator alone has 1.15 × 10⁹ non-zeros and 18.5 GB per ion (2026-09-04 critique, measured). Every validation result in Section 9 is produced in this mode, with the resolved-mode set and the frozen-spectator contribution stated per test (Section 9 preamble).
- **GATE_LOCAL.** Used when the joint space is too large. For each pulse, the simulator builds the exact joint space of the addressed ions (plus any ions that receive crosstalk light above a threshold) and all modes with non-negligible coupling to them, initializes the motional part from the tracked motional model, evolves exactly, and then extracts (a) the reduced operation on the addressed ions' internal states as a completely positive map by state-based process tomography: 16 linearly independent pure internal inputs for two qubits are propagated as state vectors (`mcsolve` when channels are active, with the trajectory count fixed from a stated map-accuracy rule, n_traj ≈ 1/ε_map per input state so that the multinomial error of each output's populations sits below the target ε_map, the sixteen population `e_ops` passed explicitly because `mcsolve` raises `ValueError` on `target_tol` without `e_ops`, and `mesolve` as the deterministic cross-check where the gate-local dimension is below about 100), the Choi matrix is reconstructed by least squares and projected onto the intersection of the completely positive cone and the trace-preserving affine set by alternating (Dykstra) projection with the residual against both constraints reported, because a positive-semidefinite projection alone leaves Tr_out(Choi) ≠ 𝟙 and the extracted map then changes the register's normalization on every application (2026-09-04 numerics critique) **[corrected: critique, 2026-09-04]**, the map is applied to the register, by Kraus sampling when the register is a pure-state ensemble, and the extraction is recomputed per quasi-static sample and cached by (pulse parameters, motional-model hash, noise sample), so that GATE_LOCAL costs 16 × n_traj state evolutions of the gate-local space, a few hundred trajectories per input at ε_map = 10⁻³ and therefore more than one `mesolve` of the same space where `mesolve` fits (the second revision counted 16; 2026-09-04 numerics critique), which is what Section 11.2 charges; and (b) the updated motional model: the reduced density matrix of every mode that was in the pulse's joint space, an (n_max + 1)² matrix per mode and so cheap to carry, from which the mean occupation, the Fock distribution and the residual coherent displacement follow, together with the untouched thermal states of the modes that were not in the space. The N-qubit register is carried as a density matrix for N ≤ 12 or as a stochastic pure-state ensemble beyond that, and the map is applied to it. The approximation is that spin-motion and mode-mode correlations left after a pulse are traced out rather than carried to the next pulse; the loss is quantified by the residual displacement |α_m(τ)| per spin eigenstate and by the purity deficit of the reduced motional state that the extraction reports, and by direct comparison with JOINT_EXACT on 3- and 4-ion circuits in the test suite.
- Both levels share prep, readout, noise sampling and result formats. A result records which level ran. Replaying derived per-gate channels for large circuits is not a core fidelity level: the separate application package of Section 14 builds that on its own side from the channel summaries the core already exports (Section 6.8), and the core neither knows nor needs to know about it.

### 5.5 Convergence and truncation policy

- The truncation monitor records, for each mode and each pulse, the population in the top two Fock levels (or the top ENR shell). If it exceeds a threshold (default 10⁻⁶ of the population the pulse moves, with a per-test override for hot-mode cases, since a thermal tail at n̄ = 20 falls below 10⁻⁶ only near n ≈ 275), or if the cap's margin above the populated range falls below the Section 5.1.1 margin for the pulse's η, the run is repeated with the cap raised, up to a configured limit, and the final report states both the boundary population and the margin reached.
- Tolerance convergence: a run can be asked to repeat with tolerances tightened by a factor of 10 and to report the change in final probabilities; the test suite does this for every validation case.
- Time-step convergence for array coefficients: programmed envelopes are sampled at 20 times their bandwidth and interpolated with the default cubic spline; noise realizations are generated by the exact Ornstein-Uhlenbeck update on an independent fixed grid with Δt ≤ τ_c/10, stored in the run record and interpolated with `order=1` (never on the solver's accepted steps, which `dop853` chooses adaptively, so that a realization generated there could not be reproduced; 2026-09-04 architect critique), because a cubic spline through a rough sample path injects spectral content the process does not have, and a test compares the realized coefficient's periodogram with the configured spectrum over the band the pulse is sensitive to; the schedule module checks both.

### 5.6 Units and constants

Internal units are SI with angular frequencies in rad/s and time in seconds; the Hamiltonian is divided by ħ so that all energies are rad/s. User-facing APIs accept frequencies in Hz (cycles) with `2π` applied internally, and document it at every entry point, because factor-of-2π errors are the single most common bug in this domain. Physical constants come from CODATA with their source recorded in `units.py`; atomic masses from the AME tables with citation in each species module.

### 5.7 The governing equations in one place

Everything the simulator integrates is one master equation on the joint space of Section 5.1,

ρ̇ = −i[H(t), ρ] + Σ_k 𝒟[L_k]ρ,  𝒟[L]ρ = LρL† − ½{L†L, ρ},  H(t) = H_phys(t)/ħ in rad/s (Section 5.6), L_k = √γ_k A_k in s^{−1/2},

(the terms listed below are written as H_phys with their ħ; the second revision wrote −(i/ħ)[H, ρ] one paragraph after declaring H already divided by ħ, a units-layer slip caught by the derivation audit of 2026-09-04) run as `sesolve`/`mcsolve` on pure states or `mesolve` on ρ (Section 5.3), with the terms below; each points to the subsection that specifies and tags it, so this list is an index, not a new claim. Every term is switchable and every switch is recorded in the run's diagnostics.

**Hamiltonian, H(t) = H_mot + H_int + Σ_i H_drive,i(t) + Σ_i H_Stark,i(t) + H_anh + H_curv.**

- H_mot = Σ_m ħω_m a_m†a_m: the pseudopotential-frame modes of Section 4.1.3 with the calibrated (noise-shifted) frequencies of 4.1.5, the micromotion factor C₀ already inside every η as `Crystal.lamb_dicke` delivers it (4.1.1; never reapplied here), and per-sample frequency offsets from the quasi-static noise of 6.2.
- H_int = Σ_i (ħΔ_i/2) σ_z^i (generalized to Σ_p ħΔ_{i,p}|p⟩⟨p| when d > 2): the offset of ion i's true transition frequency from the frame frequency, from the magnetic-field sample of 6.3, the ac Zeeman shift of 4.3.3 and the Zeeman structure of 4.5.
- H_drive,i(t) = (ħ/2) Σ_tones Ω_{i,tone}(t) e^{−i(μ_tone t − φ_tone(t))} σ₊^i ⊗ Π_m D_m(iη_{i,m}) + h.c. (4.3.1, 5.2), with Ω from the beam intensities and dipole matrix elements of 4.3.2 and 4.5; the same term on a neighbour j with Ω → ε_{ij}Ω is addressing crosstalk (6.6); the coefficient multiplied by e^{iβ_mm cos(Ω_rf t + δ)} is excess micromotion (4.1.1, 4.3.6); Ω_i → Ω_i(1 + (Ω″/2Ω)x̂_i²) is beam-curvature coupling (6.2); a sampled φ(t) or Ω(t) time series is laser phase or intensity noise (6.3, 6.4); the hardware chain of 7.10 shapes Ω(t) and quantizes μ and φ. When d > 2, σ₊ becomes the set of transition operators |p⟩⟨q| with the pair's own Rabi frequency (4.5).
- H_Stark,i(t) = (ħ/2) δ_St,i(t) σ_z^i, proportional to the instantaneous intensity (4.3.2); the compensating third tone of 4.4.1 is an ordinary drive term.
- H_anh: the cubic Coulomb coupling of 4.1.4, opt-in because it couples all three mode families into one space; the resonance checker and the estimated accumulated phase are on by default.
- H_curv: the static-field anharmonicity and mode-mode resonances of 4.1.4, optional.
- The explicit rf Hamiltonian of 4.1.1 (Landa's construction) replaces H_mot as a validation option only.

**Collapse operators L_k.**

- Heating: √(Γ(N̄+1)) a_m and √(ΓN̄) a_m† per mode (4.1.5, 6.2).
- Motional dephasing (fast part): √(2/τ_m) a_m†a_m (6.2).
- Qubit dephasing (fast part): √(γ_φ/2) σ_z^i, under which the coherence decays at γ_φ, so γ_φ = 1/T₂ for the white component (Section 13); the slow, quasi-static part is a sampled Δ_i in H_int, and a measured T₂ is split into the two before either is used (6.3).
- Photon scattering: √Γ_{i: p→q} |q⟩⟨p| ⊗ R_k for every Raman channel p → q, including channels out of the qubit pair when d > 2, and the elastic (Rayleigh) channel with its differential-amplitude dephasing (4.3.2, 4.5, 6.5), where R_k is the recoil operator of 4.2.8 (a displacement D(ik_photon·x̂) averaged over the dipole emission pattern) or the identity when recoil is switched off.
- Shelf decay during readout: √(1/τ_D) |S⟩⟨D| (8.1).
- Photon counting for time-resolved readout trajectories: the fluorescence collapse operator of 8.2.

**Stochastic elements, sampled once per dynamical sample from one seeded generator (3.1, 6.1):** mode-frequency offsets, qubit-frequency offsets with their inter-ion correlation length, Rabi-frequency scale factors, beam and beat-note phases, frozen spectator Fock states (5.2), and the time series for laser phase and intensity noise.

**Initial state (4.2):** ρ(0) = ⊗_i ρ_int,i ⊗ ⊗_m ρ_th,m(n̄_m), with ρ_int,i the optical-pumping output including its preparation error and ρ_th,m the per-mode thermal (or level-B Fock-distribution) state of the cooling model.

**Measurement (8.2-8.4):** the joint internal outcome is sampled from the final joint state as a projective measurement in the computational basis, so that Bell-state correlations survive; each ion's photon record is then generated conditioned on that outcome, with neighbour crosstalk coupling the records (8.5), and discriminated into a bit. The product POVM of 8.4 is the summary of the record and discriminator layers, computed once per device by the calibration layer, and the fast path that applies it directly to the joint state replaces those layers rather than preceding them, so the readout error is never applied twice (the first version of this plan listed the POVM before the record layer; caught by the 2026-09-04 critique). The fast path's domain is stated: the product POVM is exact only at zero readout crosstalk, because Section 8.5 makes the records neighbour-coupled (added counts on a dark neighbour, camera PSF leakage, neighbour-conditioned likelihoods), so the full path's joint confusion matrix is not a tensor product of per-ion POVMs; at non-zero crosstalk the fast path carries a register-wide 2^N × 2^N confusion tensor with its own size guard (dense to N = 12, factored by neighbour range beyond), and Section 9.5's test is agreement at zero crosstalk plus a bounded, reported discrepancy at the configured crosstalk (2026-09-04 architect critique) **[corrected: critique, 2026-09-04]**.


## 6. Noise and error models

### 6.1 Principles

Every error channel in the simulator is a physical process with a rate or spectrum derived from device parameters, entering the dynamics in one of four ways: (a) as a term in the Hamiltonian (crosstalk light, Stark shifts, beam-curvature coupling, coherent over-rotation); (b) as a Lindblad collapse operator (heating, fast dephasing, photon scattering, shelf decay); (c) as a quasi-static parameter sampled once per dynamical sample (slow magnetic-field drift, mode-frequency drift, Rabi-frequency drift, beam-phase drift); (d) as a stochastic time series in a drive or Hamiltonian coefficient (laser phase and intensity noise with a given spectrum, and the qubit-frequency and mode-frequency trajectories of Sections 6.2 and 6.3, including deterministic periodic components such as mains harmonics with a per-shot line-trigger phase); (e) as a discrete event process, the background-gas collisions of Section 6.7 that heat, reorder or lose ions and are reported as heralded or discarded shots. The choice among (b), (c) and (d) for a given physical noise is set by its correlation time relative to the gate: white noise is Lindblad, noise slow compared with a shot is quasi-static, anything in between is a sampled process (the band from 1 Hz to 10 kHz, where every laboratory's magnetic dephasing and every decoupling sequence live, is (d), not (c)). Phenomenological circuit-level channels (depolarizing, Pauli-twirled over-rotation) are not used inside the simulator; they are what the simulator produces when asked to summarize its own behaviour for a circuit-level client (Section 6.8). Every rate carries its provenance (apparatus, species, chain length), because the published numbers come from different machines: Fang's 614 quanta/s centre-of-mass heating and 8(1) ms motional coherence on a five-ion chain, Cetina's 88(6) quanta/s with S_E ∝ ω^{−0.8(1)} and no absolute normalization on a 15-ion chain, Trout's simulated 25 quanta/s. Fed into Sørensen-Mølmer's or Ballance's heating formula, Lechner's 65 quanta/s gives an error of 8 × 10⁻³ for a 250 µs two-ion gate, ten to seventy times the fidelities quoted alongside it, which is what mixing apparatus does. An N-ion error budget assembled from them is stitched from at least three apparatus, and the report says so **[extracted]**.

### 6.2 Motional channels

**Heating.** Collapse operators √(Γ(N̄+1)) a_m and √(ΓN̄) a_m† with Γ_h = ΓN̄ from S_E(ω_m) (Section 4.1.5) **[verified]**. The two closed forms the tests reproduce: ε_h = ṅ t_g/(2K) for a K-loop geometric-phase gate coupled to an infinite-temperature bath (Ballance 2016 supplement; adopted by Trout 2018 as an XX Kraus channel with p_h = r_heat t_MS) **[verified]**, and ΔF = Γ_h t_g/2 for the single-loop Mølmer-Sørensen gate (Kirchmair 2009 after Sørensen-Mølmer 2000) **[verified]**; Sørensen-Mølmer's own treatment gives the displaced jump operators and the resulting J_y-basis dephasing rate ∝ (M_y − M_y′)²Γ(1 + 2n_th)(G² + F²)/4 **[verified]**. Ballance states the closed form only for ε_h ≪ 0.1, a caveat Trout drops, and Trout's K is quoted inconsistently between sections (1 and 2 in one, 2 and 4 in another), so the simulator carries K per pulse solution and refuses the closed form outside its stated range **[corrected]**. Multi-ion correlation of the noise (uniform versus uncorrelated fields) selects which modes heat (Section 4.1.5); Cetina et al. show that under uniform-field heating the lowest axial mode dominates, with the chain's beam-curvature dephasing (below) growing as t_w²N^{4+2α} **[verified]**.

**Motional dephasing.** Two representations. Lindblad: L = a†a√(2/τ), which makes the coherence of |n⟩ + |n′⟩ decay at (n − n′)²/τ and gives a gate error ε_d = α_K t_g/τ with α_K = (8K + 3)/(16K²) = {0.6875, 0.2969, 0.1367} for K = {1, 2, 4} (Ballance 2016 prints 0.686, 0.297, 0.137; Section 4.4.4) **[verified]**, **[corrected: derivation audit, 2026-09-04]**; the simulator uses this normalization and calibrates τ from a motional Ramsey or, as Srinivas et al. did, from squeezing analysis **[corrected minor]**. Quasi-static: per-sample mode-frequency offsets drawn from the drift distribution, which is what Hughes et al. model as Ĥ_e = ħε(t)a†a with the exact two-term infidelity, thermal spin-motion (2n̄ + 1)|α_t|²λ² plus a temperature-insensitive gate-angle term **[verified]**, and what FM/AMFM stabilization is designed against **[verified]**. Srinivas's anchor: motional coherence time 64 ms costing 5.8 × 10⁻⁴ on a 740 µs gate; frequency feedforward residual σ ≈ 50 Hz costing 3 × 10⁻⁵ **[corrected minor]**.

**Spectator modes and Debye-Waller factors** need no separate channel in JOINT_EXACT mode: they are in the Hamiltonian. The closed forms they must reproduce are Bermudez et al.'s ε_m (Section 4.4.7), Landsman's δF = (4/5)Σ_{j,m}|α_{j,m}|²(2n̄_m + 1) **[verified]**, Sørensen-Mølmer's F = 1 − π²N(N−1)η⁴Var(n)/8 **[verified]**, and Ballance's ε_n̄ = (1/4)π²η⁴n̄(2n̄ + 1) **[verified]** (n̄(2n̄ + 1) is ⟨n²⟩ of the thermal state, not its variance). In GATE_LOCAL mode the dropped modes' contribution is estimated from these forms and added to the report. The cubic Coulomb coupling of Section 4.1 is a further coherent motional channel, present in no published budget, whose leading off-resonant effect is a second-order dispersive shift of order g²/Δ_res, of order 10⁻² rad or less over published gate times (Section 4.1.4; the first version of this plan reported a first-order phase g·t of 0.1 to 0.9 rad, corrected on 2026-09-04); it is an opt-in Hamiltonian term whose second-order estimate and resonance distance are reported by default **[corrected]**. Mode-frequency drift has two components with different correlation structure, and the distinction is the whole point of the FM and AM-FM robustness the plan reproduces: a common-mode fractional drift of every rf-derived mode of a family from rf-amplitude drift (ω_sec ∝ q ∝ V_rf, so 10⁻⁴ in V_rf moves every radial mode by 300 Hz at 3 MHz, the common δ against which ∂α_k/∂δ is nulled), and a per-mode differential term from dc drift; each has a correlation time and is sampled as a trajectory or a quasi-static value by the rule of Section 6.1, never as M independent offsets, because independent sampling makes closure errors average as 1/√M instead of adding and lets robustness tests pass for the wrong reason. rf amplitude and phase noise spectra S_V(ω) and S_φ(ω) are device parameters that drive the Ω_rf ± ω_t sideband heating of Section 4.1.5 and a parametric-heating term at 2ω_sec (added after the 2026-09-04 experimentalist critique) **[corrected]**.

**Beam-curvature dephasing** (Cetina et al. 2022) **[verified]**: an ion oscillating along the chain axis through a tightly focused addressing beam samples the beam's field-amplitude curvature, Ω̄_i = Ω_{i,0} + ½Ω″_{i,0}Σ_m b_{im}²E_m/(Mω_m²); with thermal mode energies this gives the decay parameter θ_{im} = −b_{im}²ξ_m²(Ω″_{i,0}/Ω_{i,0})n̄_m, a carrier Rabi curve p₁(t) = [1 − C cos(Ω_{i,0}t − φ)]/2 with C = Π_m(1 + θ_{im}²Ω²t²)^{−1/2} and φ = Σ_m arctan(θ_{im}Ωt) (a phase lag; the source's Eq. 2 prints the wrong sign **[corrected]**), and a two-qubit bound F_ij = ½ + ½Π_m[1 + (N_gπ/2)²(θ_{im} + θ_{jm})²]^{−1/2} after N_g gates **[verified]**. For a Gaussian beam Ω″/Ω = −(2/w²)(1 − 2x²/w²) **[verified]**. This is not a Debye-Waller effect (the beams are perpendicular to the motion) **[corrected]**; the simulator implements it exactly by making Ω depend on the axial position operator, Ω(x̂) = Ω₀(1 + (Ω″/2Ω₀)x̂²), which couples the drive to a†a of the axial modes, and reproduces the closed forms as tests. This mechanism and the spectator-mode Debye-Waller factor of Section 4.3 are distinct sources of shot-to-shot Rabi-frequency inhomogeneity: the Debye-Waller factor involves the modes along Δk with exponent η²(n̄ + ½) and a thermal Fock distribution; the curvature term involves the modes perpendicular to the beam with exponent κ = b²ξ²(Ω″/Ω)n̄, about 120 times smaller than a k-vector reading would give, and exponentially distributed classical mode energies with an algebraic contrast envelope. Neither is Gaussian or Markovian. They get two separate per-shot samplers on disjoint mode sets, both feeding the same frozen-Ω propagation, and neither may stand in for the other **[corrected]**.

### 6.3 Qubit dephasing

Magnetic-field noise shifts a hyperfine level by g_F μ_B m_F/h, 1.4 MHz/G per unit m_F for g_F = 1 (the F = 1 manifold of 171Yb+), so that a Δm_F = ±1 transition from the clock state shifts by 1.4 MHz/G and a Zeeman qubit between m_J = ±½ levels by 2.8 MHz/G (the first version of this plan wrote 2.8 MHz/G per unit m_F; caught by the 2026-09-04 critique), and clock qubits only quadratically: written as δν = c₂(δB)², c₂ = 310.8 Hz/G² for 171Yb+ at zero field and 1.21 mHz/mG² for the 43Ca+ |4,0⟩ ↔ |3,+1⟩ transition at 146.094 G, whose second derivative d²ν/dB² = 2c₂ is Harty's printed 2.4 mHz/mG² (the second revision paired the two numbers as if they shared a convention, which the derivation audit of 2026-09-04 caught; the Section 13 'curvature naming' row is the rule) **[corrected: derivation audit, 2026-09-04]**; both are outputs of the hyperfine-Zeeman diagonalization of Section 4.5.1 (310.85 Hz/G² and c₂ = 1.2077 mHz/mG², d²ν/dB² = 2.415 mHz/mG², recomputed), so a field sample is converted to per-level frequency offsets through computed sensitivities. Slow noise is quasi-static: per-sample frequency offsets drawn from the measured or configured distribution, correlated across ions with a correlation length ξ_c so that the gate error goes as Nt_g/(2T₂) (uncorrelated) or N²t_g/(4T₂) (global), with T₂ the single-ion 1/e coherence time (Bermudez 2017 prints 2t_gN/T₂ and 2t_gN²/T₂, 4 and 8 times high; Section 4.4.7) **[corrected: derivation audit, 2026-09-04]**; fast noise is a Lindblad σ_z term; and between them the qubit frequency has a sampled trajectory Δ_i(t) on the array-coefficient machinery of Section 6.1's option (d), built from S_B(ω) through the computed sensitivities plus a deterministic periodic component (amplitude per mains harmonic at 50 or 60 Hz and its multiples) with a per-shot line-trigger phase, uniform when the sequence is not line-synchronized and fixed when it is, because that band dominates magnetic dephasing in every laboratory and is where every decoupling sequence operates, so that Ramsey revivals, the sequence-length dependence of echo and the T₂ of (XY)^N decoupling are reproducible; the validation is a filter-function calculation of T₂ under (XY)^N for the configured spectrum, on the machinery and the χ = (2/π)∫₀^∞(dω/ω²)S_b F normalization of Section 6.9 (Section 9.7), and the Egan and Harty anchors below are compared only against a spectrum that contains their mains lines (the first version of this plan had no representation of this band; caught by the 2026-09-04 experimentalist critique) **[corrected]**. Anchors: T₂* = 610(120) ms from an un-echoed Raman Ramsey on the centre ion of a 15-ion chain, attributed to control noise; T₂ = 2.84(16) s with Gaussian decay under microwave (XY)^N decoupling (Egan 2021) **[verified]**; 50(10) s for a 43Ca+ clock qubit without shielding (Harty 2014) **[verified]**; > 0.5 s for Debnath's 5-ion chain **[verified]**; and Crain's Gaussian spectator dephasing during readout (Section 8.5). Microwave gates at the intrinsic-dynamical-decoupling point J₀(4Ω_μ/δ) = 0 suppress low-frequency dephasing by construction **[verified]**. Laser phase noise matters for phase-sensitive gates only (Section 4.4.2); it is represented as an Ornstein-Uhlenbeck realization of φ(t) in the drive coefficient with the configured linewidth, and its effect is checked against the Kirchmair anchor of 2 × 10⁻³ per gate from incoherent carrier excitation **[corrected minor]** and the Baldwin filter-function form ε = ∫S_φ(ω)F(ω)dω scaled by (4g²/Δ²)² **[extracted]**.

### 6.4 Amplitude and control errors

Slow Rabi-frequency variation is a per-sample scale factor (δΩ/Ω ≈ 1.4 × 10⁻² in Kirchmair 2009 **[verified]**; ≲ 5 × 10⁻⁴ in Harty 2014 **[verified]**); fast intensity noise with zero-frequency density Γ_I gives ε_I ≈ Γ_I t_g η²(n̄ + ½) + Γ_I t_g η² · 3(N − 1)/(8K) (Bermudez 2017 prints (N − 1)/4 for the second term; Section 4.4.7) **[corrected: derivation audit, 2026-09-04]**. Static pulse-area and detuning errors are the composite-pulse model R = exp[−(i/2)θ(1 + ε)(σ_φ + δZ)] (Mount 2015) **[verified]**, which is Section 4.3.5's primitive M(θ, φ; ε_a, ε_d) with ε = ε_a and δ = ε_d, and whose frequency-resolved counterpart, together with the quasi-static-versus-sampled decision of Section 6.1, is Section 6.9; the frequency-comb beat notes at δ′ and 2δ′ with weights 1 : 2 : 1 are included for comb-driven devices **[verified]**. Coherent over-rotation of any gate, ε_G(ρ) = e^{−iθG}ρe^{iθG}, is the model Egan et al. used to reproduce their logical-qubit data **[verified]**; the simulator produces it naturally from a miscalibrated pulse area, and the Pauli-twirled equivalent p_xx = sin²α (Trout 2018) **[verified]** is a reporting option, not a simulation primitive. Timing: Harty's 14 µs dead times between pulses and the +4.5 Hz deliberate detuning are the kind of scheduler detail the error model must carry to reproduce randomized benchmarking at the 10⁻⁶ level **[verified]**.

### 6.5 Photon scattering and leakage

Rates from Section 4.3.2 (Ozeri 2005, 2007) **[verified]**, computed for every species from the amplitude sums of Section 4.5.5: Raman scattering as spin-flip Lindblad operators between qubit levels and into the other hyperfine or Zeeman levels (leakage, requires d > 2, with the leaked levels' readout polarity taken from the manifold), Rayleigh scattering as a motional recoil kick (the recoil operator of Section 4.2.8, a displacement averaged over the emission pattern) with no internal-state effect when the two qubit states' elastic amplitudes coincide, as they do for clock qubits far from the fine-structure levels **[verified]**, the differential-Rayleigh dephasing channel of Section 4.5.5 when they do not (Uys et al. 2010, Run 4), and D-level scattering with branching f as leakage to a shelved level that the readout model then treats as dark or bright according to the species **[verified]**. The error per π pulse is P_total = (πγ/ω_f)(2Δ² + (Δ − ω_f)²)/|Δ(Δ − ω_f)| with ε_S = P_Raman **[verified]**. The photon-scattering line was the largest term in Ballance's budget (0.4 × 10⁻³) **[verified]** and second in Pino's (1.5 × 10⁻³) **[extracted]**.

### 6.6 Addressing crosstalk

Individually addressed beams leak onto neighbours. The crosstalk ratio is a Rabi-frequency ratio ε_ij = Ω_j/Ω_i measured by driving single-qubit gates on ion i (1-4% in Fang et al.'s 5-ion 171Yb+ chain; below 0.5% two sites away), and the leaked drives add as vectors in the spin plane so the spectator's effective Rabi frequency and phase are Ω_jσ_{φ_j}^{(j)} = ε_{1j}Ω₁σ_{φ₁}^{(j)} + ε_{2j}Ω₂σ_{φ₂}^{(j)} with φ₁ = 0, φ₂ = φ_beam **[verified]** (the index convention is beam i onto ion j **[corrected]**). During an MS gate the leading-order effect is U_xtalk = XX(θ)·exp[−iΣ_{j≠1,2}(θ_{1j}X^{(1)}σ_{φ_j}^{(j)} + θ_{2j}X^{(2)}σ_{φ_j}^{(j)})] **[verified]**; the printed closed forms for the Bell fidelity and the spectator excitation are off by a factor 2 inside the cosines relative to the paper's own Eq. 1. The exact forms are F = cos²θ₁₃ cos²θ₂₃ and P_ion3 = [1 − cos(2θ₁₃)cos(2θ₂₃)]/2, which give 0.900790 and 0.097217 at (θ₁₃, θ₂₃) = (0.1644, −0.2763), independent of φ_beam, against the printed 0.974422 and 0.025450; at θ = π/2 the exact form gives P_ion3 = 0 where the printed one gives 0.5. The small-angle infidelity coefficient is therefore θ², not θ²/4, a factor 4 in every crosstalk budget built on the printed forms **[corrected]**. Because φ_beam drifts on hundreds of milliseconds **[corrected]**, it is a quasi-static sample. Suppression schemes are scheduler options: neighbour suppression (split the gate, Z(π) on affected spectators) and local suppression (split the gate, Y(π) or Z(π) on the two targets), both exact to first order **[verified]**. The simulator implements crosstalk exactly by adding the leaked drive terms to the Hamiltonian; Wright et al.'s minimal model (3% bit flips into |0⟩ applied twice for Bernstein-Vazirani; 1% either way applied five times for hidden shift; 0.2% detection misidentification) is a test of what emerges **[corrected]**. Parallel gates on two pairs incur an additional commuting unitary with ½‖ℰ‖◇ ≤ Σ|Θ_{rs}| over the four inter-pair phases, which fall as 1/n³ with separation (Landsman 2019) **[corrected: the ½ is load-bearing]**; the bound is vacuous for closely spaced pairs because ‖ℰ‖◇ ≤ 2 always, so the simulator reports the exact simulated channel rather than the bound whenever the bound exceeds 1 **[corrected]**. Beam-curvature dephasing (Section 6.2) and readout crosstalk (Section 8.5) complete the addressing picture; the intensity crosstalk of integrated waveguides (0.26-0.36% at 200 µm) is the corresponding number for photonic delivery **[extracted]**.

### 6.7 Idle time, memory and preparation

Between pulses the state evolves under H₀ with heating, motional dephasing and qubit dephasing active; identity gates in benchmarking are delays (Harty) **[verified]**. Preparation error comes from the optical-pumping model (Section 4.2.6) and is reported as part of SPAM; Egan's single-ion budget (bright-state error 0.71(4)% dominated by bright-to-dark pumping 0.55% in a 100 µs window; dark-state error 0.22(2)% from dark-to-bright pumping 0.13% and background 0.07%) is a validation target for the readout model of Section 8 **[verified]**. Two SPAM figures coexist in the same papers: Egan and Wright each report about 99.3% from randomized-benchmarking fits and 99.80% from a one-sided microwave measurement, and their algorithm budgets use the former; mixing them changes a ten-qubit budget by about 5%, so every SPAM number in the results object is labelled with the definition it follows **[corrected]**.

**Background-gas collisions and crystal events** (added after the 2026-09-04 experimentalist critique) **[background]**, **[recomputed here]**. None of the channels above lets a shot fail the way real shots fail. A Langevin collision with background gas occurs at the rate Γ_L = n_bg k_L per ion with k_L = (e/(2ε₀))√(α_p/μ_r) (α_p the neutral's SI polarizability, μ_r the reduced mass); for H₂ (polarizability volume 0.80 Å³) on a heavy ion k_L = 1.5 × 10⁻⁹ cm³ s⁻¹, so at 10⁻¹¹ torr (n_bg = 3.2 × 10⁵ cm⁻³ at 300 K) Γ_L = 4.8 × 10⁻⁴ s⁻¹ per ion, one event per 35 minutes per ion and one per minute for a 30-ion chain (`check_collisions.py`). The device carries a pressure, a gas composition and outcome probabilities, and the collision channel is a Poisson process (Section 6.1's option (e)) with three outcomes: a heating kick drawn from a configured energy distribution whose scale is the neutral's thermal energy times the mass ratio, tens to thousands of quanta, which melts the crystal and is followed by recooling within the sequence's cooling stages; a reorder, sampled from a configured permutation distribution when the kick exceeds the ordering barrier, which permutes the ion labels and with them every b_{i,m}, every η_{i,m} and the pair sign s of Section 7.7, so that a crystal that reorders mid-sequence runs the rest of the sequence with the wrong mode structure until the next calibration detects it; and a loss or dark-ion event, molecule formation or, for 171Yb+, population of the F₇/₂ level (cleared at 638.6 nm, Section 4.5), which leaves the ion dark until repumped or reloaded. Results carry per-shot heralds (collision event, all-dark detection, count anomaly) and a discarded-shot count; a transport failure or an undetected ion swap under milestone M12 (Section 4.6) is the same channel with an unknown rate, since the published QCCD data removed such shots rather than modelling them **[corrected]**, so that the shot distribution has the outlier tail a laboratory sees; the melt and recrystallization dynamics themselves are a non-goal (Section 1.3), the event being instantaneous with its outcome distribution.

### 6.8 Reporting to circuit-level clients

The simulator can summarize any gate's simulated channel as (i) an average gate infidelity from process tomography of the simulated pulse, (ii) a Pauli-twirled channel, and (iii) a depolarizing rate ε with the normalization Λ_ε(ρ) = (1 − ε)ρ + ε/(4ⁿ − 1)Σ_{P≠I}PρP, in which ε equals the entanglement infidelity (Chen et al. 2023) **[verified]**. Bermudez et al.'s three-variant mapping of ε onto Pauli channels for fault-tolerance studies is provided as well **[verified]**. Reference points for such summaries: Trout et al.'s five-channel model at its fault-tolerance operating point (single-qubit over-rotation 10⁻⁴, two-qubit 10⁻³, heating 25 quanta/s as ṅ/2K, background depolarizing 8 × 10⁻⁴, dephasing 15 s⁻¹, gate time t_g = 10 + 38d µs with d the ion separation) **[verified]**; Chen et al.'s Forte medians (single-qubit 2.0 × 10⁻⁴, two-qubit 46.4 × 10⁻⁴ by direct RB, worst pair 885 × 10⁻⁴, no measurable dependence on ion separation) **[verified]**; and the error budgets tabulated in Section 4.4.7.

### 6.9 Dynamical decoupling and the filter-function description of dephasing and amplitude noise

Sections 6.2 to 6.4 give each channel a rate or a spectrum and Section 6.1 routes it into the dynamics. This subsection adds how a **control sequence** shapes its own susceptibility to a given spectrum, so that a decoupling sequence, a composite pulse or an idle is scored analytically against S_B(ω) or an amplitude spectrum before any trajectory is propagated. Run 5 read Green, Sastrawan, Uys and Biercuk 2013, Kabytayev et al. 2014 and Biercuk et al. 2009 for this layer and repaired six printed defects; the normalization is the most dangerous thing in the topic, so the pieces below travel together and are asserted against each other by a test.

**The toggling frame and the first-order overlap.** Split H(t) = H_c(t) + H₀(t) with H₀(t) = β(t)·σ a classical, zero-mean, wide-sense-stationary c-number field carrying **no** factor ½ on the Pauli vector, and factor the propagator exactly as U(t) = U_c(t)Ũ(t) with U_c the noise-free control propagator. Then H̃₀(t) = β(t)R(t)·σ with **R_ij(t) = ½Tr[U_c†(t)σ_i U_c(t)σ_j]**, a real element of SO(3) - strictly the adjoint of U_c†, not of U_c, so it is implemented from the trace formula and never from prose; the check script confirms R R^T = I with det = +1 and that for U_c = exp(−iΩtσ_x/2) the third row is (0, sin Ωt, cos Ωt) **[verified]**, **[recomputed here]**. Under piecewise-constant control R(t) = Σ_l G^{(l)}(t)R^{P_l}(t − t_{l−1})Λ^{(l−1)}, with G^{(l)} the segment boxcar, R^{P_l} the per-pulse matrix relative to the pulse start and Λ^{(l−1)}_ij = ½Tr[Q_{l−1}†σ_i Q_{l−1}σ_j] the adjoint of the propagator accumulated **before** segment l; Λ right-multiplies R^{P_l}, and substituting Q_l for Q_{l−1} or writing Λ·v for v·Λ each mis-computes the first Magnus term by about 3 × 10⁻³, the size of the noise itself **[verified]**. The frequency-domain matrix carries an **explicit −iω**: R_ij(ω) = −iω∫₀^τ dt R_ij(t)e^{+iωt}. Truncating at the first Magnus term and averaging then gives **1 − F_av ≃ (1/2π)∫_{−∞}^{∞}(dω/ω²)Σ_{ijk}S_ij(ω)R_jk(ω)R*_ik(ω)** (Green Eq. 23), the conjugate on R_ik and **not** on R_jk, with S_ij the two-sided cross-spectrum from ⟨β_i(t₁)β_j(t₂)⟩ = (1/2π)∫dω S_ij e^{iω(t₂−t₁)} and F_av ≡ ¼⟨|Tr Ũ(τ)|²⟩ the operational fidelity, which is Section 4.3.5's F_K and floors at ½ rather than 0 **[verified]**. The 1/ω² weight and the −iω are a **matched pair**: R_jk R*_ik scales as ω², the weight cancels it, and the net weighting on the bare Fourier transform of R(t) is ω⁰, so the control factor is finite at ω = 0; dropping the explicit 1/ω² in the belief that it is already inside R is an ω² error, and a 1/ω⁴ weight appears nowhere in this literature. Everything is angular frequency with exactly one 1/(2π), so the one-sided fold carries **1/π**, never 1/(2π). For pure dephasing β = (0, 0, b(t)) and F_z^{(1)}(ω) = Σ_{i=x,y,z}|R_zi(ω)|² (the two-term |R_zy|² + |R_zz|² is specific to the source's own xy-axis pulses), whence ⟨a₁²⟩ = (1/π)∫₀^∞(dω/ω²)S_b F_z^{(1)} and 1 − F_av = ⟨a₁²⟩ + O(ξ⁴); the two quadratures add incoherently only because ⟨β_aβ_d⟩ = 0 for jointly Gaussian processes. Validity is ξ² ≪ 1 with ξ² = (τ²/π)Σ_i∫₀^{ω_ci}S_i dω plus Magnus convergence **[verified]**. Green's per-pulse closed form for the dephasing row (Eq. 40) needs an explicit ½ on its Tr(σ_{φ_l}σ_zσ_j) term that the paper drops, so the printed version overshoots both transverse components by exactly 2.0000 while R_zz is right; the corrected components match direct quadrature to 3 × 10⁻⁶¹ and reproduce the paper's own primitive-π_X forms, which alone discriminate **[corrected]**, **[recomputed here]**. Its poles at ω = ±Ω_l are removable (guard on ‖ω| − |Ω_l‖), and free evolution and a pure z rotation are the one degenerate case θ_l = 0 **and** Ω_l = 0 together, whose angle still propagates through Q_{l−1} into Λ^{(l−1)} (Section 13).

**S_b is the PSD of the σ_z coefficient, and that is a factor 4.** Fix once for the whole simulator: H_deph(t) = b(t)σ_z with **no** ½, so b(t) is **half** the qubit-splitting fluctuation and the noise-accumulated relative phase is φ(t) = 2∫₀^t b dt′; S_b is the two-sided PSD of b under the 1/(2π) inverse-transform convention. Under that reading Biercuk's printed prefactor and Green's corrected exponent are the **same equation**: **χ(τ) = (2/π)∫₀^∞(dω/ω²)S_b(ω)F(ωτ)**, W(τ) = e^{−χ}, F_av = (1 + W)/2, P_err = (1 − W)/2 and χ = 2⟨a₁²⟩, so the weak-noise linear form is 1 − F_av = ⟨a₁²⟩ = χ/2. Reading the source's S_β as the **splitting-fluctuation** PSD makes χ four times too large; each paper is self-consistent because S(Green) = S(splitting)/4 while F(Green) = 4F(Biercuk) and the factors cancel in 1 − F, so mixing the halves is the factor-4 error **[corrected]**. The end-to-end fixture is free precession with S_b(ω) = √(2π)(δb²/σ)exp[−ω²/(2σ²)] at σ = 2.0 rad/s and δb = 0.3, the rms of the σ_z coefficient, with τ = 1 s and F = 4sin²(ωτ/2): C(0) = 0.09 = δb², ⟨a₁²⟩ = 0.0687560089446 from the spectral integral against 0.0687560089446 from the direct double integral of the autocorrelation, W = e^{−2⟨a₁²⟩} = 0.871524 (the printed e^{−⟨a₁²⟩} = 0.933554 under-predicts the exponent by 2), Biercuk's (2/π)∫ = 0.137512 = 2⟨a₁²⟩ exactly, and 0.550048 under the splitting-PSD misreading **[recomputed here]**. Green's Eqs. 35a and 35b as printed expand to ⟨a₁²⟩/2, contradicting the same paper's Eqs. 16, 18 and 34, so the module uses the linear form inside ξ² ≪ 1 and the corrected exponential only for long-time extrapolation **[corrected]**. A supplied qubit-frequency spectrum S_δ for a Hamiltonian δ(t)σ_z/2 converts as S_b = S_δ/4.

**The pulse-train filter function, its parities and its orders.** For a net-identity sequence of n finite-duration π_X pulses at fractional centres δ_j in a total time τ that **includes** the pulse durations, with dephasing gated off during each pulse, F(ωτ) = |1 + (−1)^{n+1}e^{iωτ} + 2cos(ωτ_π/2)Σ_{j=1}^{n}(−1)^j e^{iδ_jωτ}|² (Biercuk Eq. 2) **[verified]**. The whole finite-pulse modification relative to the ideal-pulse filter functions is that one cos(ωτ_π/2) factor, and the (−1)^{n+1} makes the expression valid for **both** parities; Green's Eq. 45 is the bang-bang even-n twin, whose explicit 1 − e^{iωτ} presumes Λ^{(n)} = I, so at odd n it returns |R_zz(0)|² = 4 exactly, no low-frequency suppression at all **[corrected]**, **[recomputed here]**. The simulator therefore builds F from the (−1)^{n+1} form, whose limits are 4sin²(ωτ/2) for free induction and 16sin⁴(ωτ/4) for the Hahn echo **[verified]**, **[recomputed here]**. Timings are **CPMG** δ_j = (2j − 1)/2n, exact and τ_π-independent under the pulse-inclusive convention, and **UDD** δ_j = sin²[πj/(2n + 2)], which coincide at n = 1 and 2 and first differ at n = 3; both satisfy A₁ = Σ_j(−1)^jδ_j = ∓½, which a scheduler must preserve when rounding pulse centres to a clock grid, and feasibility needs δ_π ≤ 2sin²[π/(2n + 2)]. In the bang-bang limit F ∝ (ωτ)^{2(α+1)} with τ the sequence time (τ_π → 0 there, so τ_π cannot appear; the second revision printed ωτ_π; 2026-09-04 theorist critique), that is 6(α + 1) dB/octave on the power convention: **Carr-Purcell** cancels R_zz to third order in the Taylor sense for every even n, so F ∼ ω⁶, α = 2, 18 dB/octave, while **UDD** cancels the first n derivatives, so R_zz ∼ ω^{n+1}, F ∼ ω^{2(n+1)}, α = n, with F → (ωτ)^{2n+2}/(16^n(n!)²) reproduced to 12 digits for n = 1 to 8 **[verified]**, **[recomputed here]**. Those α values are **bang-bang idealizations, not properties of the timings**: primitive π_X pulses of non-zero width already drop Carr-Purcell from α = 2 to 1, and under the finite-τ_π filter function the UDD derivative cancellation itself collapses - at δ_π = τ_π/τ = 0.02 the low-frequency order of F drops from 2n + 2 to **4 for every odd n and 6 for every even n** from n = 3 upward, so n = 12 gives 6 and not 26, with the **universal odd-n floor F → (ωτ_π)⁴/16**, a coefficient independent of n and of the pulse placement, and (ωτ)²(ωτ_π)⁴/64 for even n; UDD n = 1 and 2 are unaffected, coinciding with CPMG, and for CPMG the finite-τ_π term interferes with a surviving ideal-pulse term so its coefficient is not universal **[verified]**, **[recomputed here]**. Verifying these orders needs arbitrary precision, F being an O(1) sum cancelling to ω^{2n+2}, so the test runs in mpmath. Two scheduler consequences: a sequence's dominant noise band must never sit at ωτ_π = π, where the cosine annihilates every pulse term and F collapses to the free-induction form; and with S_b ∝ ω⁻⁴ the integrand goes as ω^{2α−4}, so χ is infrared-divergent for free induction, the Hahn echo and every odd-n sequence and converges only from even n ≥ 2 (measured χ(10⁻⁸)/χ(10⁻⁷) = 9.9986 and 9.9998 for UDD n = 3 and 5, against exactly 1.0 at n = 4 and 6) **[recomputed here]**. No source supplies an infrared cutoff, so ω_min is the simulator's choice, tied to the total experiment duration and printed alongside every χ (Section 12).

**The amplitude-noise filter function and its dc condition.** For additive amplitude noise β_a(t) on a piecewise-constant drive the control vector is ρ_a(t) = ½Σ_l G^{(l)}(t)ρ̂(φ_l)Λ^{(l−1)}, **constant within a segment** because ρ̂(φ_l)·σ commutes with the intra-segment propagator, which is why the amplitude filter function is pulse-shape independent at fixed total time and the detuning one is not, and F_a(ω) = ¼{|Σ_l A_l(ω)ρ̃_a^{(l)}|² + |Σ_l B_l(ω)ρ̃_a^{(l)}|²} exactly, with A_l = cos ωt_l − cos ωt_{l−1}, B_l = sin ωt_l − sin ωt_{l−1} and ρ̃_a^{(l)} = ρ̂(φ_l)Λ^{(l−1)} **[verified]**; no time integration is needed, and the ¼ inherited from the ½ in ρ_a must be kept or every infidelity inflates fourfold. First-order dc amplitude cancellation **is** the vanishing of Σ_l θ_l ρ̃_a^{(l)}, geometrically a closed polygon of toggled axes (a triangle for SK1, two opposite-signed triangles for BB1); the script gets |Σ| ≤ 2.7 × 10⁻⁶⁰ for SK1, BB1, PB1, SCROFULOUS and both reduced concatenations at θ = π and (π, 0, 0) for a primitive π pulse and for CORPSE, which corrects the other quadrature **[verified]**, **[recomputed here]**. The primitive filter function is exactly F_a^P(ω) = sin²(ωτ_P/2), rising as ω², while **every** amplitude-correcting composite pulse gives ω⁴ - BB1's extra dc order buys a prefactor about 3.4 times smaller than SK1's, not a higher filter order **[verified]**, **[recomputed here]**. The printed (1/16)(ωτ_CP)⁴ is an upper **bound**, not an equality (it assumes all ρ̃_a^{(l)} equal and sits 7 times above SK1 and 25 above BB1 at ω = 10⁻⁴Ω), so the crossover above which a composite pulse **hurts** is under-estimated by the bound's 2τ_P/τ_CP² = 0.025465Ω against true 0.070293Ω for SK1 and 0.132464Ω for BB1 at θ = π **[corrected]**, **[recomputed here]**; composite pulses therefore stay useful up to amplitude fluctuations of order 10% of the Rabi frequency and not above.

**The dc floor and the max rule.** The first-order filter function **under-estimates** the error for ω/Ω ≪ 1, exactly the quasi-static 1/f regime that dominates trapped-ion amplitude and dephasing noise, because it omits the higher Magnus orders that survive when the noise is frozen over the sequence. For an mth-order sequence under frozen noise 1 − F ≈ **ĉ_{m+1}⟨(β_μ(0)/Ω)^{2(m+1)}⟩** with ĉ dimensionless and sequence- and axis-dependent, ⟨β^{2(m+1)}⟩ = (2m + 1)!!⟨β²⟩^{m+1} for Gaussian noise and ⟨β²⟩ = (1/π) × the one-sided power. The **normalization trap** is the worst in this topic: ĉ is a pure number only when paired with the **dimensionless relative** error β/Ω, because the source prints Φ₂ and ĉ₂ in Ω = 1 units, so pairing the printed ĉ₂ = 22.83 with a moment in (rad/s)⁴ errs by Ω^{2(m+1)} - at the paper's own Ω = 1.5 × 10⁶ rad/s a factor 5.06 × 10²⁴, giving an infidelity of 2.97 × 10¹⁹ **[corrected]**, **[recomputed here]**. Equivalently store c_{m+1} = ĉ_{m+1}/Ω^{2(m+1)} with physical β. Calibrated against the source's own figure the corrected normalization gives an SK1 floor of 5.8736 × 10⁻⁶ against 5.86 × 10⁻⁶ read off the plot, excluding three alternative normalizations that give 1.5 × 10⁻⁶, 2.3 × 10⁻⁴ and 5.8 × 10⁻⁵; BB1 recomputes to 3.5368 × 10⁻⁹ against a quoted 3.9 × 10⁻⁹ and CORPSE to 1.6725 × 10⁻⁹ against a quoted 3.0 × 10⁻⁹, so those two published floors are **not** reproduced and the computed coefficients are what the module carries **[corrected]**, **[recomputed here]**. Cross-quadrature sequences add a term ĉ_{1,1}⟨β_a²β_d²⟩/Ω⁴ left as an unfilled placeholder in the source and computed here. **The prescription the module copies is to evaluate both estimates and report the larger at every point, 1 − F = max[(1 − F)_FF, (1 − F)_dc]**; without the dc floor the simulator reports spuriously low infidelities for precisely the band that dominates a real trap **[verified]**.

**How this attaches to the plan, and what the sources do not supply.** Section 6.1's routes become quantitative: route (c), a quasi-static sample, is the dc-floor limit and is right when the sequence's crossover frequency lies above the noise band, while route (d), a sampled trajectory, is what the filter function describes, and the two must agree in the overlap - a Monte Carlo over the same S_b, propagated with the Section 4.3.1 Hamiltonian and averaged as ¼|Tr[U_c†U]|², is the check that closes the loop and the one test here that exercises the whole path rather than a closed form. Section 6.3's magnetic-field trajectory Δ_i(t) is exactly the S_b this subsection consumes once the factor 4 above is applied, and the Section 9.7 row for (XY)^N decoupling is evaluated as W = e^{−χ} rather than by an exponential-decay fit, the Egan T₂ = 2.84(16) s and Harty T₂* = 50(10) s anchors being compared only against a spectrum containing their mains lines. Section 6.4's static (ε_a, ε_d) pair and Section 4.3.5's library are the ω → 0 end of the same object: a composite pulse's order is the low-frequency roll-off of its filter function, and the dc floor is what that order claim degrades to once higher Magnus terms are kept. Two things this layer needs are **absent from every source in the set**, and Section 12 lists the rest. Multi-axis dynamical decoupling (XY4, XY8, KDD, concatenated CDD) alternates the pulse axis, and every closed form above assumes each pulse is a π rotation about the **same** axis - which is exactly what collapses Λ^{(l)} to the scalar (−1)^l.
